I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, work is the thing I can point to most easily because it has actual spreadsheets and emails attached to it, if that makes sense. So when I try to explain it, I end up talking about work.

I don’t know… maybe? I mean, errands and plans don’t stand out to me the same way work does. Like if I’m at the grocery store and I’m comparing two brands of something, I might get stuck for a minute, but then I think everybody does that, right?

I’ll go to run an errand and then remember I didn’t reply to someone, or I’ll be making a plan and suddenly I’m thinking about my calendar at work, and then it all blends together. So I can’t honestly say, yes, I’m anxious about errands for no reason. It’s more like there’s just this background feeling, and I don’t always know what it’s attached to.

I haven’t really paid attention in categories like that. It all just feels like noise most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about everyday situations — like running errands, making plans, or small decisions — even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I’ll go to run an errand and then remember I didn’t reply to someone, or I’ll be making a plan and suddenly I’m thinking about my calendar at work, and then it all blends together. So I can’t honestly say, yes, I’m anxious about errands for no reason. It’s more like there’s just this background feeling, and I don’t always know what it’s attached to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Even social stuff—I don’t have some clear panic about it, it’s more like I’ll overthink whether I sounded weird, but then I do that with emails too, so it all blurs together.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering the exact question. But that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I don’t see people and think, “I’m worried right now,” if that makes sense. Sometimes I’m just kind of tense or distracted, but I couldn’t swear it’s not work in the back of my mind somewhere. It’s like even if I’m not actively thinking about work, it could be sitting there underneath.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t come up with a clean example where I know for sure it had nothing to do with work.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of a time in the last week when you felt anxious or worried and you were not at work, not thinking about work, and not around anyone who reminded you of work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. In the last week… I’m trying to pin it down, and it’s hard because everything sort of overlaps. Like, I was at home one evening and I remember feeling uneasy, but then I was also looking at my phone, and my phone has emails on it, and then suddenly I’m thinking maybe that counts as work even if I wasn’t “at work.”

And then there was another time I was making dinner, but I was trying to use up vegetables before they went bad, and I got annoyed because I always buy spinach thinking I’ll be a person who eats spinach every day, and then it turns into this whole guilt thing about wasting food… sorry, that’s not really your question.

So I don’t know. I can’t think of a clean example where I can honestly say I was worried and it had nothing to do with work or anything connected to it. I might have felt tense, but I don’t know what it was attached to.

I can’t think of a clean example where I can honestly say I was worried and it had nothing to do with work or anything connected to it. I might have felt tense, but I don’t know what it was attached to.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you weren’t near your phone, weren’t at work, and weren’t talking to anyone related to work—like maybe when you were out for a walk, in the shower, or doing something completely unrelated?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m trying to think… like the shower should be easy, right, because I’m not near my phone, but I honestly don’t know what I’m thinking about in there half the time. Sometimes I’m just going through the motions, and then I realize I’ve been standing there too long and the water’s getting cold, and then I’m annoyed at myself because the utility bill is already ridiculous—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

A walk… I don’t really take many walks just as walks. If I’m out, I’m usually going somewhere, or I’m thinking about what I have to pick up, or whether I locked the door, or whatever. But I don’t know if I’d call that anxious exactly. It’s more like… my body feels wound up sometimes, but I couldn’t point to a clean moment and say, yes, that was anxiety and it definitely wasn’t connected to work or my phone or something.

So I guess no, I can’t think of a specific example like that.

I’ll get into bed and just lie there replaying client models in my head, like numbers and assumptions and all the little things that could go wrong. And then if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to be able to push through work stuff—finance is always like that, deadlines and projections and people wanting certainty where there isn’t any—but now I go into work feeling like I never actually rested. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your trouble sleeping started before your feelings of anxiety, after them, or about the same time?\n\nPatient: I think… it was the sleep that really made me notice it, or at least that’s what pushed me to come in. I’d get into bed and my brain would go straight back to the client models—assumptions, numbers, all the ways something could be wrong—and then if I did fall asleep I’d wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight.\n\nSo it’s hard to separate, because the worry is kind of there in the lying awake, but the “I’m barely sleeping in any useful way anymore” part was what started feeling impossible. Then I’d go to work feeling like I hadn’t actually rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a lot of different things, even when there isn’t a specific problem at work or home, or is it mainly work-related concerns that keep coming up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, work is the thing I can point to most easily because it has actual spreadsheets and emails attached to it, if that makes sense. So when I try to explain it, I end up talking about work.\n\nBut then sometimes I can’t tell if it’s really only work or if work is just… the easiest container for it. Like even on a quiet evening, I might still feel that hum in the background, but then I’ll start thinking about a client call from three days ago or something I need to fix in a model, so it loops back there.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I guess I haven’t really tracked whether it’s “a lot of different things” or mostly work. Work is what keeps coming up when I try to name it.\n\nDoctor: When you say there’s a ‘hum in the background’ even on quiet evenings, does that ever involve worrying about things outside of work—like relationships, health, or other responsibilities—or does it mostly stay focused on work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can sort it that neatly. I mean, if you ask “relationships or health,” my mind kind of blanks, because I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m worried about my health,” you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something, and there’s this pressure in my chest, and then my brain grabs onto whatever is easiest to justify it. Usually that’s work, because there’s always some client thing or a model assumption or an email I should’ve answered better.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means it’s only work. I haven’t really paid attention in categories like that. It all just feels like noise most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about everyday situations — like running errands, making plans, or small decisions — even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, errands and plans don’t stand out to me the same way work does. I’ll go to run an errand and then remember I didn’t reply to someone, or I’ll be making a plan and suddenly I’m thinking about my calendar at work, and then it all blends together. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… lying in bed is probably the closest thing. Especially at night. I’ll get in bed and I’m supposed to be done, you know, like the day is over, but my brain just keeps pulling up client models and little details from them, like I’m checking them in my head even though there’s nothing I can do about it at that hour.

And then mornings are bad too, but in this weird way where I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 and my stomach is already tight, like I’ve been bracing for something. Sometimes I just lie there and think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get two hours… and then it becomes math, which is ridiculous because I do enough of that all day.

That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I go to work feeling like I never really rested.

So when I try to explain it, I end up talking about work.\n\nBut then sometimes I can’t tell if it’s really only work or if work is just… the easiest container for it. Like even on a quiet evening, I might still feel that hum in the background, but then I’ll start thinking about a client call from three days ago or something I need to fix in a model, so it loops back there.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I guess I haven’t really tracked whether it’s “a lot of different things” or mostly work. Work is what keeps coming up when I try to name it.\n\nDoctor: When you say there’s a ‘hum in the background’ even on quiet evenings, does that ever involve worrying about things outside of work—like relationships, health, or other responsibilities—or does it mostly stay focused on work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can sort it that neatly. I mean, if you ask “relationships or health,” my mind kind of blanks, because I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m worried about my health,” you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something, and there’s this pressure in my chest, and then my brain grabs onto whatever is easiest to justify it. Usually that’s work, because there’s always some client thing or a model assumption or an email I should’ve answered better.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means it’s only work. I haven’t really paid attention in categories like that. It all just feels like noise most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about everyday situations — like running errands, making plans, or small decisions — even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, errands and plans don’t stand out to me the same way work does. Like if I’m at the grocery store and I’m comparing two brands of something, I might get stuck for a minute, but then I think everybody does that, right?\n\nIt’s hard because I don’t really separate “small decisions” from the rest of the day. I’ll go to run an errand and then remember I didn’t reply to someone, or I’ll be making a plan and suddenly I’m thinking about my calendar at work, and then it all blends together. So I can’t honestly say, yes, I’m anxious about errands for no reason. It’s more like there’s just this background feeling, and I don’t always know what it’s attached to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about everyday things like social interactions, minor obligations, or small future events — even when there’s no clear reason or consequence tied to them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that specifically. Like, if I know I have to call someone back or respond to an invitation, I might put it off, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… I don’t know, being tired of dealing with things.\n\nSmall future events, maybe, but then I start thinking about my calendar and whether I’ve forgotten something at work, and it sort of turns into that again. Even social stuff—I don’t have some clear panic about it, it’s more like I’ll overthink whether I sounded weird, but then I do that with emails too, so it all blurs together.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering the exact question. I haven’t really separated it out that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt anxious or worried when you weren’t thinking about work at all — like when you were with friends, watching something, or doing something just for yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and my mind keeps going to… well, like, if I’m watching a show, I’m not sure I’m really watching it. I’ll be halfway through an episode and realize I missed some important plot thing, and then I’m annoyed because now I have to rewind, and then I think about how I used to actually enjoy shows more. But that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I don’t see people and think, “I’m worried right now,” if that makes sense. Sometimes I’m just kind of tense or distracted, but I couldn’t swear it’s not work in the back of my mind somewhere. It’s like even if I’m not actively thinking about work, it could be sitting there underneath.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t come up with a clean example where I know for sure it had nothing to do with work.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the last week when you felt anxious or worried and you were not at work, not thinking about work, and not around anyone who reminded you of work?\n\nPatient: In the last week… I’m trying to pin it down, and it’s hard because everything sort of overlaps. Like, I was at home one evening and I remember feeling uneasy, but then I was also looking at my phone, and my phone has emails on it, and then suddenly I’m thinking maybe that counts as work even if I wasn’t “at work.”\n\nAnd then there was another time I was making dinner, but I was trying to use up vegetables before they went bad, and I got annoyed because I always buy spinach thinking I’ll be a person who eats spinach every day, and then it turns into this whole guilt thing about wasting food… sorry, that’s not really your question.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t think of a clean example where I can honestly say I was worried and it had nothing to do with work or anything connected to it. I might have felt tense, but I don’t know what it was attached to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you weren’t near your phone, weren’t at work, and weren’t talking to anyone related to work—like maybe when you were out for a walk, in the shower, or doing something completely unrelated?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… like the shower should be easy, right, because I’m not near my phone, but I honestly don’t know what I’m thinking about in there half the time. Sometimes I’m just going through the motions, and then I realize I’ve been standing there too long and the water’s getting cold, and then I’m annoyed at myself because the utility bill is already ridiculous—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nA walk… I don’t really take many walks just as walks. If I’m out, I’m usually going somewhere, or I’m thinking about what I have to pick up, or whether I locked the door, or whatever. But I don’t know if I’d call that anxious exactly. It’s more like… my body feels wound up sometimes, but I couldn’t point to a clean moment and say, yes, that was anxiety and it definitely wasn’t connected to work or my phone or something.\n\nSo I guess no, I can’t think of a specific example like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you were completely alone, not thinking about work, not around other people, and not dealing with any specific task or responsibility—like maybe first waking up in the morning or just lying in bed before sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lying in bed is probably the closest thing. Especially at night. I’ll get in bed and I’m supposed to be done, you know, like the day is over, but my brain just keeps pulling up client models and little details from them, like I’m checking them in my head even though there’s nothing I can do about it at that hour.\n\nAnd then mornings are bad too, but in this weird way where I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 and my stomach is already tight, like I’ve been bracing for something. Sometimes I just lie there and think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get two hours… and then it becomes math, which is ridiculous because I do enough of that all day.\n\nThat’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I go to work feeling like I never really rested.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think so… not in that totally blank way you’re describing. I mean, I’m trying to picture it, but if I’m tense, there’s usually some thought hovering around, even if it’s stupid or tiny.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m not really sleeping anymore—not in a way that feels useful. I’ll get into bed and just lie there replaying client models in my head, like numbers and assumptions and all the little things that could go wrong. And then if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to be able to push through work stuff—finance is always like that, deadlines and projections and people wanting certainty where there isn’t any—but now I go into work feeling like I never actually rested. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your trouble sleeping started before your feelings of anxiety, after them, or about the same time?\n\nPatient: I think… it was the sleep that really made me notice it, or at least that’s what pushed me to come in. I’d get into bed and my brain would go straight back to the client models—assumptions, numbers, all the ways something could be wrong—and then if I did fall asleep I’d wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight.\n\nSo it’s hard to separate, because the worry is kind of there in the lying awake, but the “I’m barely sleeping in any useful way anymore” part was what started feeling impossible. Then I’d go to work feeling like I hadn’t actually rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a lot of different things, even when there isn’t a specific problem at work or home, or is it mainly work-related concerns that keep coming up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, work is the thing I can point to most easily because it has actual spreadsheets and emails attached to it, if that makes sense. So when I try to explain it, I end up talking about work.\n\nBut then sometimes I can’t tell if it’s really only work or if work is just… the easiest container for it. Like even on a quiet evening, I might still feel that hum in the background, but then I’ll start thinking about a client call from three days ago or something I need to fix in a model, so it loops back there.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I guess I haven’t really tracked whether it’s “a lot of different things” or mostly work. Work is what keeps coming up when I try to name it.\n\nDoctor: When you say there’s a ‘hum in the background’ even on quiet evenings, does that ever involve worrying about things outside of work—like relationships, health, or other responsibilities—or does it mostly stay focused on work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can sort it that neatly. I mean, if you ask “relationships or health,” my mind kind of blanks, because I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m worried about my health,” you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something, and there’s this pressure in my chest, and then my brain grabs onto whatever is easiest to justify it. Usually that’s work, because there’s always some client thing or a model assumption or an email I should’ve answered better.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means it’s only work. I haven’t really paid attention in categories like that. It all just feels like noise most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about everyday situations — like running errands, making plans, or small decisions — even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, errands and plans don’t stand out to me the same way work does. Like if I’m at the grocery store and I’m comparing two brands of something, I might get stuck for a minute, but then I think everybody does that, right?\n\nIt’s hard because I don’t really separate “small decisions” from the rest of the day. I’ll go to run an errand and then remember I didn’t reply to someone, or I’ll be making a plan and suddenly I’m thinking about my calendar at work, and then it all blends together. So I can’t honestly say, yes, I’m anxious about errands for no reason. It’s more like there’s just this background feeling, and I don’t always know what it’s attached to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about everyday things like social interactions, minor obligations, or small future events — even when there’s no clear reason or consequence tied to them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that specifically. Like, if I know I have to call someone back or respond to an invitation, I might put it off, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… I don’t know, being tired of dealing with things.\n\nSmall future events, maybe, but then I start thinking about my calendar and whether I’ve forgotten something at work, and it sort of turns into that again. Even social stuff—I don’t have some clear panic about it, it’s more like I’ll overthink whether I sounded weird, but then I do that with emails too, so it all blurs together.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering the exact question. I haven’t really separated it out that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt anxious or worried when you weren’t thinking about work at all — like when you were with friends, watching something, or doing something just for yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and my mind keeps going to… well, like, if I’m watching a show, I’m not sure I’m really watching it. I’ll be halfway through an episode and realize I missed some important plot thing, and then I’m annoyed because now I have to rewind, and then I think about how I used to actually enjoy shows more. But that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I don’t see people and think, “I’m worried right now,” if that makes sense. Sometimes I’m just kind of tense or distracted, but I couldn’t swear it’s not work in the back of my mind somewhere. It’s like even if I’m not actively thinking about work, it could be sitting there underneath.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t come up with a clean example where I know for sure it had nothing to do with work.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the last week when you felt anxious or worried and you were not at work, not thinking about work, and not around anyone who reminded you of work?\n\nPatient: In the last week… I’m trying to pin it down, and it’s hard because everything sort of overlaps. Like, I was at home one evening and I remember feeling uneasy, but then I was also looking at my phone, and my phone has emails on it, and then suddenly I’m thinking maybe that counts as work even if I wasn’t “at work.”\n\nAnd then there was another time I was making dinner, but I was trying to use up vegetables before they went bad, and I got annoyed because I always buy spinach thinking I’ll be a person who eats spinach every day, and then it turns into this whole guilt thing about wasting food… sorry, that’s not really your question.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t think of a clean example where I can honestly say I was worried and it had nothing to do with work or anything connected to it. I might have felt tense, but I don’t know what it was attached to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you weren’t near your phone, weren’t at work, and weren’t talking to anyone related to work—like maybe when you were out for a walk, in the shower, or doing something completely unrelated?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… like the shower should be easy, right, because I’m not near my phone, but I honestly don’t know what I’m thinking about in there half the time. Sometimes I’m just going through the motions, and then I realize I’ve been standing there too long and the water’s getting cold, and then I’m annoyed at myself because the utility bill is already ridiculous—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nA walk… I don’t really take many walks just as walks. If I’m out, I’m usually going somewhere, or I’m thinking about what I have to pick up, or whether I locked the door, or whatever. But I don’t know if I’d call that anxious exactly. It’s more like… my body feels wound up sometimes, but I couldn’t point to a clean moment and say, yes, that was anxiety and it definitely wasn’t connected to work or my phone or something.\n\nSo I guess no, I can’t think of a specific example like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you were completely alone, not thinking about work, not around other people, and not dealing with any specific task or responsibility—like maybe first waking up in the morning or just lying in bed before sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lying in bed is probably the closest thing. Especially at night. I’ll get in bed and I’m supposed to be done, you know, like the day is over, but my brain just keeps pulling up client models and little details from them, like I’m checking them in my head even though there’s nothing I can do about it at that hour.\n\nAnd then mornings are bad too, but in this weird way where I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 and my stomach is already tight, like I’ve been bracing for something. Sometimes I just lie there and think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get two hours… and then it becomes math, which is ridiculous because I do enough of that all day.\n\nThat’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I go to work feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt physically tense or anxious without any clear reason or trigger—like nothing specific was happening, you weren't thinking about work or responsibilities, and there was no obvious cause for the feeling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. Like, if I’m tense, there’s usually something my mind attaches to it after the fact, even if it wasn’t the original reason. I’ll think, well, maybe it’s because I forgot something, or because the house is messy, or because I need to return that library book—actually I don’t even use the library much anymore, I just have this one book that’s been sitting there forever.\n\nSo… without any clear reason? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in a way where I could say, yes, nothing was happening and nothing was on my mind. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt physically tense or anxious and there was absolutely nothing specific on your mind—no work, no responsibilities, no thoughts about the past or future—and the feeling still came up for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that totally blank way you’re describing. I mean, I’m trying to picture it, but if I’m tense, there’s usually some thought hovering around, even if it’s stupid or tiny.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there and think I’m not thinking about anything, but then I realize I’m noticing the laundry basket, or the dent in the wall I never fixed, or I’m wondering why the refrigerator makes that sound now. Actually, that reminds me I still haven’t called the landlord about it, but—sorry.\n\nSo no, I can’t really recall a time where my mind was completely empty and the feeling just appeared out of nowhere. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you were completely alone, not thinking about work, not around other people, and not dealing with any specific task or responsibility—like maybe first waking up in the morning or just lying in bed before sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lying in bed is probably the closest thing. Especially at night. I’ll get in bed and I’m supposed to be done, you know, like the day is over, but my brain just keeps pulling up client models and little details from them, like I’m checking them in my head even though there’s nothing I can do about it at that hour.\n\nAnd then mornings are bad too, but in this weird way where I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 and my stomach is already tight, like I’ve been bracing for something. Sometimes I just lie there and think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get two hours… and then it becomes math, which is ridiculous because I do enough of that all day.\n\nThat’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I go to work feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt physically tense or anxious without any clear reason or trigger—like nothing specific was happening, you weren't thinking about work or responsibilities, and there was no obvious cause for the feeling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. Like, if I’m tense, there’s usually something my mind attaches to it after the fact, even if it wasn’t the original reason. I’ll think, well, maybe it’s because I forgot something, or because the house is messy, or because I need to return that library book—actually I don’t even use the library much anymore, I just have this one book that’s been sitting there forever.\n\nSo… without any clear reason? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in a way where I could say, yes, nothing was happening and nothing was on my mind. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt physically tense or anxious and there was absolutely nothing specific on your mind—no work, no responsibilities, no thoughts about the past or future—and the feeling still came up for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that totally blank way you’re describing. I mean, I’m trying to picture it, but if I’m tense, there’s usually some thought hovering around, even if it’s stupid or tiny.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there and think I’m not thinking about anything, but then I realize I’m noticing the laundry basket, or the dent in the wall I never fixed, or I’m wondering why the refrigerator makes that sound now. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you recall a specific time when you were completely relaxed and doing something enjoyable, with no work, responsibilities, or tasks on your mind, and yet you suddenly felt a wave of anxiety or physical tension for no apparent reason?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 48 / financial_or_investment_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of an example, but… I don’t know that I have one like that. Completely relaxed and enjoying something with nothing else on my mind—that’s already kind of hard for me to picture.

Like, I might be watching a show or making tea or something, but then I’m noticing the emails I didn’t answer, or the cup I left on the counter, or I start thinking about whether I paid the insurance bill. Not even in a dramatic way, just… it sneaks in.

So if I’ve felt tense during something that was supposed to be relaxing, I don’t think I can honestly say it came out of nowhere. There’s usually some little thread attached to it, even if I only notice it afterward.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m not really sleeping anymore—not in a way that feels useful. I’ll get into bed and just lie there replaying client models in my head, like numbers and assumptions and all the little things that could go wrong. And then if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to be able to push through work stuff—finance is always like that, deadlines and projections and people wanting certainty where there isn’t any—but now I go into work feeling like I never actually rested. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your trouble sleeping started before your feelings of anxiety, after them, or about the same time?\n\nPatient: I think… it was the sleep that really made me notice it, or at least that’s what pushed me to come in. I’d get into bed and my brain would go straight back to the client models—assumptions, numbers, all the ways something could be wrong—and then if I did fall asleep I’d wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight.\n\nSo it’s hard to separate, because the worry is kind of there in the lying awake, but the “I’m barely sleeping in any useful way anymore” part was what started feeling impossible. Then I’d go to work feeling like I hadn’t actually rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a lot of different things, even when there isn’t a specific problem at work or home, or is it mainly work-related concerns that keep coming up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, work is the thing I can point to most easily because it has actual spreadsheets and emails attached to it, if that makes sense. So when I try to explain it, I end up talking about work.\n\nBut then sometimes I can’t tell if it’s really only work or if work is just… the easiest container for it. Like even on a quiet evening, I might still feel that hum in the background, but then I’ll start thinking about a client call from three days ago or something I need to fix in a model, so it loops back there.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I guess I haven’t really tracked whether it’s “a lot of different things” or mostly work. Work is what keeps coming up when I try to name it.\n\nDoctor: When you say there’s a ‘hum in the background’ even on quiet evenings, does that ever involve worrying about things outside of work—like relationships, health, or other responsibilities—or does it mostly stay focused on work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can sort it that neatly. I mean, if you ask “relationships or health,” my mind kind of blanks, because I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m worried about my health,” you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something, and there’s this pressure in my chest, and then my brain grabs onto whatever is easiest to justify it. Usually that’s work, because there’s always some client thing or a model assumption or an email I should’ve answered better.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means it’s only work. I haven’t really paid attention in categories like that. It all just feels like noise most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about everyday situations — like running errands, making plans, or small decisions — even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, errands and plans don’t stand out to me the same way work does. Like if I’m at the grocery store and I’m comparing two brands of something, I might get stuck for a minute, but then I think everybody does that, right?\n\nIt’s hard because I don’t really separate “small decisions” from the rest of the day. I’ll go to run an errand and then remember I didn’t reply to someone, or I’ll be making a plan and suddenly I’m thinking about my calendar at work, and then it all blends together. So I can’t honestly say, yes, I’m anxious about errands for no reason. It’s more like there’s just this background feeling, and I don’t always know what it’s attached to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about everyday things like social interactions, minor obligations, or small future events — even when there’s no clear reason or consequence tied to them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that specifically. Like, if I know I have to call someone back or respond to an invitation, I might put it off, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… I don’t know, being tired of dealing with things.\n\nSmall future events, maybe, but then I start thinking about my calendar and whether I’ve forgotten something at work, and it sort of turns into that again. Even social stuff—I don’t have some clear panic about it, it’s more like I’ll overthink whether I sounded weird, but then I do that with emails too, so it all blurs together.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering the exact question. I haven’t really separated it out that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt anxious or worried when you weren’t thinking about work at all — like when you were with friends, watching something, or doing something just for yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and my mind keeps going to… well, like, if I’m watching a show, I’m not sure I’m really watching it. I’ll be halfway through an episode and realize I missed some important plot thing, and then I’m annoyed because now I have to rewind, and then I think about how I used to actually enjoy shows more. But that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I don’t see people and think, “I’m worried right now,” if that makes sense. Sometimes I’m just kind of tense or distracted, but I couldn’t swear it’s not work in the back of my mind somewhere. It’s like even if I’m not actively thinking about work, it could be sitting there underneath.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t come up with a clean example where I know for sure it had nothing to do with work.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the last week when you felt anxious or worried and you were not at work, not thinking about work, and not around anyone who reminded you of work?\n\nPatient: In the last week… I’m trying to pin it down, and it’s hard because everything sort of overlaps. Like, I was at home one evening and I remember feeling uneasy, but then I was also looking at my phone, and my phone has emails on it, and then suddenly I’m thinking maybe that counts as work even if I wasn’t “at work.”\n\nAnd then there was another time I was making dinner, but I was trying to use up vegetables before they went bad, and I got annoyed because I always buy spinach thinking I’ll be a person who eats spinach every day, and then it turns into this whole guilt thing about wasting food… sorry, that’s not really your question.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t think of a clean example where I can honestly say I was worried and it had nothing to do with work or anything connected to it. I might have felt tense, but I don’t know what it was attached to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you weren’t near your phone, weren’t at work, and weren’t talking to anyone related to work—like maybe when you were out for a walk, in the shower, or doing something completely unrelated?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… like the shower should be easy, right, because I’m not near my phone, but I honestly don’t know what I’m thinking about in there half the time. If I’m out, I’m usually going somewhere, or I’m thinking about what I have to pick up, or whether I locked the door, or whatever. But I don’t know if I’d call that anxious exactly. It’s more like… my body feels wound up sometimes, but I couldn’t point to a clean moment and say, yes, that was anxiety and it definitely wasn’t connected to work or my phone or something.\n\nSo I guess no, I can’t think of a specific example like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you were completely alone, not thinking about work, not around other people, and not dealing with any specific task or responsibility—like maybe first waking up in the morning or just lying in bed before sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lying in bed is probably the closest thing. I’m not sure I can pin down a moment like that. I used to enjoy being outside more, or doing little things that felt nice, but now even when I try, I don’t know if I’d call it “nothing was wrong.” There’s usually some kind of… background noise in my head.

I don’t really have a clean example of that. I know you’re asking about, like, no responsibilities, no work thoughts, no task in my head, and then anxiety just appearing… but I’m not sure my mind ever feels that empty.

Even if I’m technically free, like on a weekend or just sitting somewhere, I’ll still notice something I should’ve done, or I’ll remember an email, or I’ll think about a spreadsheet from work even when I’m not supposed to be. Sometimes I’ll get up to do one thing and then see the mail on the table and then I’m thinking about bills, and then the whole “relaxing” part is gone.

So… no, I can’t recall a sudden wave for no clear reason. If I feel tense, there’s usually some little thing connected to it, even if it takes me a minute to figure out what it is.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.22, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m not really sleeping anymore—not in a way that feels useful. I’ll get into bed and just lie there replaying client models in my head, like numbers and assumptions and all the little things that could go wrong. And then if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to be able to push through work stuff—finance is always like that, deadlines and projections and people wanting certainty where there isn’t any—but now I go into work feeling like I never actually rested. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your trouble sleeping started before your feelings of anxiety, after them, or about the same time?\n\nPatient: I think… it was the sleep that really made me notice it, or at least that’s what pushed me to come in. I’d get into bed and my brain would go straight back to the client models—assumptions, numbers, all the ways something could be wrong—and then if I did fall asleep I’d wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight.\n\nSo it’s hard to separate, because the worry is kind of there in the lying awake, but the “I’m barely sleeping in any useful way anymore” part was what started feeling impossible. Then I’d go to work feeling like I hadn’t actually rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a lot of different things, even when there isn’t a specific problem at work or home, or is it mainly work-related concerns that keep coming up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, work is the thing I can point to most easily because it has actual spreadsheets and emails attached to it, if that makes sense. So when I try to explain it, I end up talking about work.\n\nBut then sometimes I can’t tell if it’s really only work or if work is just… the easiest container for it. Like even on a quiet evening, I might still feel that hum in the background, but then I’ll start thinking about a client call from three days ago or something I need to fix in a model, so it loops back there.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I guess I haven’t really tracked whether it’s “a lot of different things” or mostly work. Work is what keeps coming up when I try to name it.\n\nDoctor: When you say there’s a ‘hum in the background’ even on quiet evenings, does that ever involve worrying about things outside of work—like relationships, health, or other responsibilities—or does it mostly stay focused on work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can sort it that neatly. I mean, if you ask “relationships or health,” my mind kind of blanks, because I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m worried about my health,” you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something, and there’s this pressure in my chest, and then my brain grabs onto whatever is easiest to justify it. Usually that’s work, because there’s always some client thing or a model assumption or an email I should’ve answered better.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means it’s only work. I haven’t really paid attention in categories like that. It all just feels like noise most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about everyday situations — like running errands, making plans, or small decisions — even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, errands and plans don’t stand out to me the same way work does. Like if I’m at the grocery store and I’m comparing two brands of something, I might get stuck for a minute, but then I think everybody does that, right?\n\nIt’s hard because I don’t really separate “small decisions” from the rest of the day. I’ll go to run an errand and then remember I didn’t reply to someone, or I’ll be making a plan and suddenly I’m thinking about my calendar at work, and then it all blends together. So I can’t honestly say, yes, I’m anxious about errands for no reason. It’s more like there’s just this background feeling, and I don’t always know what it’s attached to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about everyday things like social interactions, minor obligations, or small future events — even when there’s no clear reason or consequence tied to them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that specifically. Like, if I know I have to call someone back or respond to an invitation, I might put it off, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… I don’t know, being tired of dealing with things.\n\nSmall future events, maybe, but then I start thinking about my calendar and whether I’ve forgotten something at work, and it sort of turns into that again. Even social stuff—I don’t have some clear panic about it, it’s more like I’ll overthink whether I sounded weird, but then I do that with emails too, so it all blurs together.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering the exact question. I haven’t really separated it out that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt anxious or worried when you weren’t thinking about work at all — like when you were with friends, watching something, or doing something just for yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and my mind keeps going to… well, like, if I’m watching a show, I’m not sure I’m really watching it. I’ll be halfway through an episode and realize I missed some important plot thing, and then I’m annoyed because now I have to rewind, and then I think about how I used to actually enjoy shows more. But that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I don’t see people and think, “I’m worried right now,” if that makes sense. Sometimes I’m just kind of tense or distracted, but I couldn’t swear it’s not work in the back of my mind somewhere. It’s like even if I’m not actively thinking about work, it could be sitting there underneath.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t come up with a clean example where I know for sure it had nothing to do with work.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the last week when you felt anxious or worried and you were not at work, not thinking about work, and not around anyone who reminded you of work?\n\nPatient: In the last week… I’m trying to pin it down, and it’s hard because everything sort of overlaps. Like, I was at home one evening and I remember feeling uneasy, but then I was also looking at my phone, and my phone has emails on it, and then suddenly I’m thinking maybe that counts as work even if I wasn’t “at work.”\n\nAnd then there was another time I was making dinner, but I was trying to use up vegetables before they went bad, and I got annoyed because I always buy spinach thinking I’ll be a person who eats spinach every day, and then it turns into this whole guilt thing about wasting food… sorry, that’s not really your question.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t think of a clean example where I can honestly say I was worried and it had nothing to do with work or anything connected to it. I might have felt tense, but I don’t know what it was attached to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you weren’t near your phone, weren’t at work, and weren’t talking to anyone related to work—like maybe when you were out for a walk, in the shower, or doing something completely unrelated?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… like the shower should be easy, right, because I’m not near my phone, but I honestly don’t know what I’m thinking about in there half the time. Sometimes I’m just going through the motions, and then I realize I’ve been standing there too long and the water’s getting cold, and then I’m annoyed at myself because the utility bill is already ridiculous—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nA walk… I don’t really take many walks just as walks. If I’m out, I’m usually going somewhere, or I’m thinking about what I have to pick up, or whether I locked the door, or whatever. But I don’t know if I’d call that anxious exactly. It’s more like… my body feels wound up sometimes, but I couldn’t point to a clean moment and say, yes, that was anxiety and it definitely wasn’t connected to work or my phone or something.\n\nSo I guess no, I can’t think of a specific example like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you were completely alone, not thinking about work, not around other people, and not dealing with any specific task or responsibility—like maybe first waking up in the morning or just lying in bed before sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lying in bed is probably the closest thing. Especially at night. I’ll get in bed and I’m supposed to be done, you know, like the day is over, but my brain just keeps pulling up client models and little details from them, like I’m checking them in my head even though there’s nothing I can do about it at that hour.\n\nAnd then mornings are bad too, but in this weird way where I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 and my stomach is already tight, like I’ve been bracing for something. Sometimes I just lie there and think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get two hours… and then it becomes math, which is ridiculous because I do enough of that all day.\n\nThat’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I go to work feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt physically tense or anxious without any clear reason or trigger—like nothing specific was happening, you weren't thinking about work or responsibilities, and there was no obvious cause for the feeling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. Like, if I’m tense, there’s usually something my mind attaches to it after the fact, even if it wasn’t the original reason. I’ll think, well, maybe it’s because I forgot something, or because the house is messy, or because I need to return that library book—actually I don’t even use the library much anymore, I just have this one book that’s been sitting there forever.\n\nSo… without any clear reason? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in a way where I could say, yes, nothing was happening and nothing was on my mind. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt physically tense or anxious and there was absolutely nothing specific on your mind—no work, no responsibilities, no thoughts about the past or future—and the feeling still came up for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that totally blank way you’re describing. I mean, I’m trying to picture it, but if I’m tense, there’s usually some thought hovering around, even if it’s stupid or tiny.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there and think I’m not thinking about anything, but then I realize I’m noticing the laundry basket, or the dent in the wall I never fixed, or I’m wondering why the refrigerator makes that sound now. Actually, that reminds me I still haven’t called the landlord about it, but—sorry.\n\nSo no, I can’t really recall a time where my mind was completely empty and the feeling just appeared out of nowhere. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific time when you were completely relaxed and doing something enjoyable, with no work, responsibilities, or tasks on your mind, and yet you suddenly felt a wave of anxiety or physical tension for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of an example, but… I don’t know that I have one like that. Completely relaxed and enjoying something with nothing else on my mind—that’s already kind of hard for me to picture.\n\nLike, I might be watching a show or making tea or something, but then I’m noticing the emails I didn’t answer, or the cup I left on the counter, or I start thinking about whether I paid the insurance bill. Not even in a dramatic way, just… it sneaks in.\n\nSo if I’ve felt tense during something that was supposed to be relaxing, I don’t think I can honestly say it came out of nowhere. There’s usually some little thread attached to it, even if I only notice it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were doing something that you used to genuinely enjoy—like a hobby, being outside, or spending time with someone you care about—where you suddenly felt anxious or physically tense, even though nothing was wrong or different in that moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin down a moment like that. I used to enjoy being outside more, or doing little things that felt nice, but now even when I try, I don’t know if I’d call it “nothing was wrong.” There’s usually some kind of… background noise in my head.\n\nLike if I’m with someone I care about, I might suddenly feel tense, but then I start wondering if I said something odd, or if I’m being boring, or if I should be somewhere else. And with hobbies—well, I don’t really keep up with them the way I used to. I bought these art supplies once, thinking I’d get back into it, and they’re still in a drawer somewhere. I think one of the markers dried out before I even used it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve noticed anxiety coming out of nowhere when everything was fine. It’s more like there’s always something attached to it, even if it’s small.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were completely free of responsibilities, not thinking about work, and not focused on any specific task or problem, yet you still felt a sudden wave of anxiety or physical tension for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clean example of that. I know you’re asking about, like, no responsibilities, no work thoughts, no task in my head, and then anxiety just appearing… but I’m not sure my mind ever feels that empty.\n\nEven if I’m technically free, like on a weekend or just sitting somewhere, I’ll still notice something I should’ve done, or I’ll remember an email, or I’ll think about a spreadsheet from work even when I’m not supposed to be. Sometimes I’ll get up to do one thing and then see the mail on the table and then I’m thinking about bills, and then the whole “relaxing” part is gone.\n\nSo… no, I can’t recall a sudden wave for no clear reason. If I feel tense, there’s usually some little thing connected to it, even if it takes me a minute to figure out what it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m not sure, honestly. A completely neutral situation is hard for me to identify. Even if I’m just sitting there, supposedly not doing anything, my mind sort of fills in the blank space.

I don’t think so. I mean, “completely at ease” is the part I get stuck on, because I’m trying to think of when that even happens.

Sometimes I’ll sit on the couch with tea or something, and it should be calm, but then I notice a stack of papers on the table, or I remember I didn’t call someone back, and then my mind goes off. Or I’ll think I’m not thinking about work, but then I realize I’m mentally rearranging a report or wondering if I missed a number somewhere. It’s like when you leave a browser with too many tabs open—sorry, that’s probably not the best analogy, but that’s how it feels.

So, no, I can’t really recall a clear moment where I was totally relaxed and then anxiety just came over me out of nowhere. If I feel tense, I usually end up finding some thread attached to it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.22, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m not really sleeping anymore—not in a way that feels useful. I’ll get into bed and just lie there replaying client models in my head, like numbers and assumptions and all the little things that could go wrong. And then if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to be able to push through work stuff—finance is always like that, deadlines and projections and people wanting certainty where there isn’t any—but now I go into work feeling like I never actually rested. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your trouble sleeping started before your feelings of anxiety, after them, or about the same time?\n\nPatient: I think… it was the sleep that really made me notice it, or at least that’s what pushed me to come in. I’d get into bed and my brain would go straight back to the client models—assumptions, numbers, all the ways something could be wrong—and then if I did fall asleep I’d wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight.\n\nSo it’s hard to separate, because the worry is kind of there in the lying awake, but the “I’m barely sleeping in any useful way anymore” part was what started feeling impossible. Then I’d go to work feeling like I hadn’t actually rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a lot of different things, even when there isn’t a specific problem at work or home, or is it mainly work-related concerns that keep coming up?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, work is the thing I can point to most easily because it has actual spreadsheets and emails attached to it, if that makes sense. So when I try to explain it, I end up talking about work.\n\nBut then sometimes I can’t tell if it’s really only work or if work is just… the easiest container for it. Like even on a quiet evening, I might still feel that hum in the background, but then I’ll start thinking about a client call from three days ago or something I need to fix in a model, so it loops back there.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer. I guess I haven’t really tracked whether it’s “a lot of different things” or mostly work. Work is what keeps coming up when I try to name it.\n\nDoctor: When you say there’s a ‘hum in the background’ even on quiet evenings, does that ever involve worrying about things outside of work—like relationships, health, or other responsibilities—or does it mostly stay focused on work-related concerns?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can sort it that neatly. I mean, if you ask “relationships or health,” my mind kind of blanks, because I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m worried about my health,” you know?\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something, and there’s this pressure in my chest, and then my brain grabs onto whatever is easiest to justify it. Usually that’s work, because there’s always some client thing or a model assumption or an email I should’ve answered better.\n\nBut I don’t know if that means it’s only work. I haven’t really paid attention in categories like that. It all just feels like noise most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about everyday situations — like running errands, making plans, or small decisions — even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? I mean, errands and plans don’t stand out to me the same way work does. Like if I’m at the grocery store and I’m comparing two brands of something, I might get stuck for a minute, but then I think everybody does that, right?\n\nIt’s hard because I don’t really separate “small decisions” from the rest of the day. I’ll go to run an errand and then remember I didn’t reply to someone, or I’ll be making a plan and suddenly I’m thinking about my calendar at work, and then it all blends together. So I can’t honestly say, yes, I’m anxious about errands for no reason. It’s more like there’s just this background feeling, and I don’t always know what it’s attached to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about everyday things like social interactions, minor obligations, or small future events — even when there’s no clear reason or consequence tied to them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed it that specifically. Like, if I know I have to call someone back or respond to an invitation, I might put it off, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… I don’t know, being tired of dealing with things.\n\nSmall future events, maybe, but then I start thinking about my calendar and whether I’ve forgotten something at work, and it sort of turns into that again. Even social stuff—I don’t have some clear panic about it, it’s more like I’ll overthink whether I sounded weird, but then I do that with emails too, so it all blurs together.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering the exact question. I haven’t really separated it out that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt anxious or worried when you weren’t thinking about work at all — like when you were with friends, watching something, or doing something just for yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and my mind keeps going to… well, like, if I’m watching a show, I’m not sure I’m really watching it. I’ll be halfway through an episode and realize I missed some important plot thing, and then I’m annoyed because now I have to rewind, and then I think about how I used to actually enjoy shows more. But that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nWith friends… I don’t know. I don’t see people and think, “I’m worried right now,” if that makes sense. Sometimes I’m just kind of tense or distracted, but I couldn’t swear it’s not work in the back of my mind somewhere. It’s like even if I’m not actively thinking about work, it could be sitting there underneath.\n\nSo… I guess I can’t come up with a clean example where I know for sure it had nothing to do with work.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the last week when you felt anxious or worried and you were not at work, not thinking about work, and not around anyone who reminded you of work?\n\nPatient: In the last week… I’m trying to pin it down, and it’s hard because everything sort of overlaps. Like, I was at home one evening and I remember feeling uneasy, but then I was also looking at my phone, and my phone has emails on it, and then suddenly I’m thinking maybe that counts as work even if I wasn’t “at work.”\n\nAnd then there was another time I was making dinner, but I was trying to use up vegetables before they went bad, and I got annoyed because I always buy spinach thinking I’ll be a person who eats spinach every day, and then it turns into this whole guilt thing about wasting food… sorry, that’s not really your question.\n\nSo I don’t know. I can’t think of a clean example where I can honestly say I was worried and it had nothing to do with work or anything connected to it. I might have felt tense, but I don’t know what it was attached to.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you weren’t near your phone, weren’t at work, and weren’t talking to anyone related to work—like maybe when you were out for a walk, in the shower, or doing something completely unrelated?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… like the shower should be easy, right, because I’m not near my phone, but I honestly don’t know what I’m thinking about in there half the time. Sometimes I’m just going through the motions, and then I realize I’ve been standing there too long and the water’s getting cold, and then I’m annoyed at myself because the utility bill is already ridiculous—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nA walk… I don’t really take many walks just as walks. If I’m out, I’m usually going somewhere, or I’m thinking about what I have to pick up, or whether I locked the door, or whatever. But I don’t know if I’d call that anxious exactly. It’s more like… my body feels wound up sometimes, but I couldn’t point to a clean moment and say, yes, that was anxiety and it definitely wasn’t connected to work or my phone or something.\n\nSo I guess no, I can’t think of a specific example like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt physically tense or anxious when you were completely alone, not thinking about work, not around other people, and not dealing with any specific task or responsibility—like maybe first waking up in the morning or just lying in bed before sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lying in bed is probably the closest thing. Especially at night. I’ll get in bed and I’m supposed to be done, you know, like the day is over, but my brain just keeps pulling up client models and little details from them, like I’m checking them in my head even though there’s nothing I can do about it at that hour.\n\nAnd then mornings are bad too, but in this weird way where I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 and my stomach is already tight, like I’ve been bracing for something. Sometimes I just lie there and think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get two hours… and then it becomes math, which is ridiculous because I do enough of that all day.\n\nThat’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I go to work feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt physically tense or anxious without any clear reason or trigger—like nothing specific was happening, you weren't thinking about work or responsibilities, and there was no obvious cause for the feeling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly. Like, if I’m tense, there’s usually something my mind attaches to it after the fact, even if it wasn’t the original reason. I’ll think, well, maybe it’s because I forgot something, or because the house is messy, or because I need to return that library book—actually I don’t even use the library much anymore, I just have this one book that’s been sitting there forever.\n\nSo… without any clear reason? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in a way where I could say, yes, nothing was happening and nothing was on my mind. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you felt physically tense or anxious and there was absolutely nothing specific on your mind—no work, no responsibilities, no thoughts about the past or future—and the feeling still came up for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that totally blank way you’re describing. I mean, I’m trying to picture it, but if I’m tense, there’s usually some thought hovering around, even if it’s stupid or tiny.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there and think I’m not thinking about anything, but then I realize I’m noticing the laundry basket, or the dent in the wall I never fixed, or I’m wondering why the refrigerator makes that sound now. Actually, that reminds me I still haven’t called the landlord about it, but—sorry.\n\nSo no, I can’t really recall a time where my mind was completely empty and the feeling just appeared out of nowhere. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific time when you were completely relaxed and doing something enjoyable, with no work, responsibilities, or tasks on your mind, and yet you suddenly felt a wave of anxiety or physical tension for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of an example, but… I don’t know that I have one like that. Completely relaxed and enjoying something with nothing else on my mind—that’s already kind of hard for me to picture.\n\nLike, I might be watching a show or making tea or something, but then I’m noticing the emails I didn’t answer, or the cup I left on the counter, or I start thinking about whether I paid the insurance bill. Not even in a dramatic way, just… it sneaks in.\n\nSo if I’ve felt tense during something that was supposed to be relaxing, I don’t think I can honestly say it came out of nowhere. There’s usually some little thread attached to it, even if I only notice it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were doing something that you used to genuinely enjoy—like a hobby, being outside, or spending time with someone you care about—where you suddenly felt anxious or physically tense, even though nothing was wrong or different in that moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin down a moment like that. I used to enjoy being outside more, or doing little things that felt nice, but now even when I try, I don’t know if I’d call it “nothing was wrong.” There’s usually some kind of… background noise in my head.\n\nLike if I’m with someone I care about, I might suddenly feel tense, but then I start wondering if I said something odd, or if I’m being boring, or if I should be somewhere else. And with hobbies—well, I don’t really keep up with them the way I used to. I bought these art supplies once, thinking I’d get back into it, and they’re still in a drawer somewhere. I think one of the markers dried out before I even used it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say I’ve noticed anxiety coming out of nowhere when everything was fine. It’s more like there’s always something attached to it, even if it’s small.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were completely free of responsibilities, not thinking about work, and not focused on any specific task or problem, yet you still felt a sudden wave of anxiety or physical tension for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clean example of that. I know you’re asking about, like, no responsibilities, no work thoughts, no task in my head, and then anxiety just appearing… but I’m not sure my mind ever feels that empty.\n\nEven if I’m technically free, like on a weekend or just sitting somewhere, I’ll still notice something I should’ve done, or I’ll remember an email, or I’ll think about a spreadsheet from work even when I’m not supposed to be. Sometimes I’ll get up to do one thing and then see the mail on the table and then I’m thinking about bills, and then the whole “relaxing” part is gone.\n\nSo… no, I can’t recall a sudden wave for no clear reason. If I feel tense, there’s usually some little thing connected to it, even if it takes me a minute to figure out what it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were in a completely neutral situation—no work, no responsibilities, no specific thoughts about the past or future—and you suddenly felt anxious or physically tense for no reason at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. A completely neutral situation is hard for me to identify. Even if I’m just sitting there, supposedly not doing anything, my mind sort of fills in the blank space.\n\nLike, I’ll be in the kitchen and notice the light hitting the counter a certain way, and then somehow I’m thinking about how I never replaced the blinds, and then that reminds me of the contractor my neighbor recommended, and then I’m off somewhere else entirely. So by the time I feel tense, I don’t know if it was “for no reason” or if my brain already attached a reason to it without me noticing.\n\nSo… I don’t think I can give you a clear example of anxiety just appearing in a totally neutral moment. I haven’t really noticed it happening that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you were completely at ease, not thinking about anything in particular—no work, no responsibilities, no worries—and suddenly, for no clear reason, you felt a wave of anxiety or physical tension come over you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, “completely at ease” is the part I get stuck on, because I’m trying to think of when that even happens.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit on the couch with tea or something, and it should be calm, but then I notice a stack of papers on the table, or I remember I didn’t call someone back, and then my mind goes off. Or I’ll think I’m not thinking about work, but then I realize I’m mentally rearranging a report or wondering if I missed a number somewhere. It’s like when you leave a browser with too many tabs open—sorry, that’s probably not the best analogy, but that’s how it feels.\n\nSo, no, I can’t really recall a clear moment where I was totally relaxed and then anxiety just came over me out of nowhere. If I feel tense, I usually end up finding some thread attached to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m trying to picture that, but… no, I don’t think I can honestly point to a time like that. “Completely at rest” feels almost hypothetical to me.
